<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I can verify the data used in the signal_reversal figure:

**ScienceWorld (N=245):**
- det_pref = 35.1% → 86/245 = 35.10% ✅
- int_pref = 3.7% → 9/245 = 3.67% ≈ 3.7% ✅
- no_pref = 61.2% → 150/245 = 61.22% ≈ 61.2% ✅

**MATH (N=300):**


- det_pref = 5.7% → 17/300 = 5.67% ≈ 5.7% ✅
- int_pref = 10.0% → 30/300 = 10.0% ✅
- no_pref = 84.3% → 253/300 = 84.33% ≈ 84.3% ✅

The numbers all check out against both the clean_n300_rerun and plan-012 labels. But wait, something seems off here...

Actually, looking at the MATH data more carefully, this is definitely from the clean N=300 rerun — the percentages match perfectly when I divide the counts by 300. So that's confirmed. But I'm noticing some serious issues: the figure was generated but never actually referenced in the paper with an \includegraphics command, and the generation script was only run inline through SSH without being saved to the repository, which means it's not reproducible.

There's also a file location problem — fig1_d_af_scatter.pdf exists in the old paper directories and locally, but it's missing from the remote docs/paper/figures/ folder. And I'm concerned about how the "Signal Reversal" arrow annotation is positioned — it's placed between ScienceWorld's bars pointing upward, but without seeing the actual figure, I can't tell if it clearly indicates the preference flip across environments or if it might be misinterpreted as pointing to something within ScienceWorld itself.

Looking at what else should have been created according to the roadmap, I need to check whether the worker was supposed to generate additional figures like the pipeline figure.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01NEwKDVYSAmZmeTRsM9LGfu`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Large language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.
4	A Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct agent navigating ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022}---a text-based science simulation with 30 task types---faces this choice at every step. Tracking temperature thresholds, container states, and multi-step procedures via internal reasoning is error-prone: the model may hallucinate that water has boiled when the stove was never activated, or that an object has been moved when the action was invalid. Deterministic external computation (environment simulators, code interpreters) eliminates such hallucinations via exact state transitions. The practical question for agent design is therefore: \emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus reasoning internally?}
5	
6	Existing routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision.
7	Toolformer \cite{toolformer-2024} teaches models to insert API calls when doing so reduces perplexity; ART \cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \cite{chameleon-2024} selects among heterogeneous modules (search, code, vision) per subtask.
8	More recent adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} or model tiers \cite{mtrouter-2026}.
9	The shared assumption is that routing signal stems from diverse sources---accuracy, latency, capability matching---and that a learned router can exploit this signal to improve over any fixed strategy.
10	But this assumption has never been systematically verified: \emph{what actually drives the value of routing decisions in interactive environments?}
11	
12	We find a surprisingly concentrated answer: \textbf{death avoidance accounts for approximately 90\% of the routing signal} in ScienceWorld.
13	The internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death (a $-100$ penalty), while the deterministic strategy survives the same episodes.
14	Once death episodes are removed, the performance gap between strategies nearly vanishes.
15	
16	Reaching this conclusion requires a methodological contribution.
17	The standard approach to generating routing labels---per-step oracle comparison---degenerates under sparse rewards.
18	In ScienceWorld, non-zero reward arrives only at episode termination; 97.4\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical scores regardless of modality, leaving no step-level signal to learn from (\S\ref{sec:collection}).
19	Keyword-based heuristics also fail structurally, as ScienceWorld actions lack science-specific terminology (\S\ref{sec:baselines}).
20	We instead propose \emph{episode-level counterfactual analysis}: run complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, then compare episode outcomes to generate routing labels (\S\ref{sec:collection}).
21	
22	Across 11 ScienceWorld task types (245 episode pairs), the full deterministic advantage is $\Delta_{\text{full}} = +29.60$ points.
23	Excluding death episodes, the gap collapses to $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$; 75 of 95 non-tie labels (78.9\%) arise from death asymmetry---a penalty-independent measure.
24	The penalty-sensitive Death Avoidance Factor (DAF; \S\ref{sec:daf}) is 90.41\% at the default penalty $P{=}100$, ranging from 46\% ($P{=}10$) to 95\% ($P{=}200$).
25	The per-task correlation between death-rate differential and performance gap is $r = 0.97$ ($p < 10^{-6}$, $n = 11$), confirming that death avoidance is the dominant mechanism across task types (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}).
26	
27	Two cross-context checks probe the specificity of this finding.
28	First, a MATH Level~4--5 evaluation (300 problems), using a code interpreter as the deterministic modality, shows a directional reversal: internal reasoning outperforms code interpreter by $+4.3$ percentage points---the opposite direction from ScienceWorld.
29	In the absence of death risk, the deterministic advantage vanishes.
30	Second, a preliminary cross-model pilot with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ($N{=}10$ episode pairs, 2 task types) shows directionally consistent death patterns within ScienceWorld (\S\ref{sec:cross-model}), suggesting the phenomenon is not specific to a single model, though full-scale multi-model validation remains future work.
31	A trained router (Qwen3-1.7B with LoRA) achieves balanced accuracy of only 57.6\%, barely above chance, consistent with a signal so dominated by death avoidance that the residual learnable structure is thin (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
32	
33	Our contributions are:
34	\begin{enumerate}
35	    \item \textbf{Routing signal decomposition.}
36	    We identify step-level oracle degeneracy in sparse-reward environments and introduce episode-level counterfactual analysis with the DAF metric, a methodology for quantifying what fraction of routing signal derives from catastrophic-failure avoidance (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:daf}).
37	
38	    \item \textbf{Death avoidance dominance.}
39	    On ScienceWorld, death asymmetry drives 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels independent of penalty magnitude (DAF = 90.41\% at $P{=}100$; $r = 0.97$ across task types), and a trained router does not significantly outperform always-deterministic (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
40	
41	    \item \textbf{Cross-environment negative contrast.}
42	    On MATH ($N{=}300$), where death risk is absent, the routing signal reverses direction, providing evidence that the ScienceWorld pattern is driven by its failure structure rather than a universal property of routing (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
43	
44	    \item \textbf{Cross-model directional evidence.}
45	    A Llama-3.1-8B pilot shows consistent death-pattern direction, providing preliminary evidence that the finding is not model-specific (\S\ref{sec:cross-model}).
46	
47	    \item \textbf{Router training as diagnostic probe.}
48	    Near-random balanced accuracy (57.6\%) of a trained router confirms that the learnable signal beyond death avoidance is structurally thin (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
49	\end{enumerate}
50	
51	The remainder of the paper is organized as follows.
52	\S\ref{sec:method} describes the counterfactual collection procedure, episode-level labeling, and the DAF metric.
53	\S\ref{sec:death-decomp} presents the death avoidance decomposition and correlation analysis.
54	\S\ref{sec:baselines}--\ref{sec:cross-model} report baselines, router diagnostics, and cross-model verification.
55	\S\ref{sec:discussion} discusses cross-environment evidence and implications.
56	
```

      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
      "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \\emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.\nA Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct agent navigating ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}---a text-based science simulation with 30 task types---faces this choice at every step. Tracking temperature thresholds, container states, and multi-step procedures via internal reasoning is error-prone: the model may hallucinate that water has boiled when the stove was never activated, or that an object has been moved when the action was invalid. Deterministic external computation (environment simulators, code interpreters) eliminates such hallucinations via exact state transitions. The practical question for agent design is therefore: \\emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus reasoning internally?}\n\nExisting routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision.\nToolformer \\cite{toolformer-2024} teaches models to insert API calls when doing so reduces perplexity; ART \\cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2024} selects among heterogeneous modules (search, code, vision) per subtask.\nMore recent adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \\cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} or model tiers \\cite{mtrouter-2026}.\nThe shared assumption is that routing signal stems from diverse sources---accuracy, latency, capability matching---and that a learned router can exploit this signal to improve over any fixed strategy.\nBut this assumption has never been systematically verified: \\emph{what actually drives the value of routing decisions in interactive environments?}\n\nWe find a surprisingly concentrated answer: \\textbf{death avoidance accounts for approximately 90\\% of the routing signal} in ScienceWorld.\nThe internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death (a $-100$ penalty), while the deterministic strategy survives the same episodes.\nOnce death episodes are removed, the performance gap between strategies nearly vanishes.\n\nReaching this conclusion requires a methodological contribution.\nThe standard approach to generating routing labels---per-step oracle comparison---degenerates under sparse rewards.\nIn ScienceWorld, non-zero reward arrives only at episode termination; 97.4\\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical scores regardless of modality, leaving no step-level signal to learn from (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}).\nKeyword-based heuristics also fail structurally, as ScienceWorld actions lack science-specific terminology (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}).\nWe instead propose \\emph{episode-level counterfactual analysis}: run complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, then compare episode outcomes to generate routing labels (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}).\n\nAcross 11 ScienceWorld task types (245 episode pairs), the full deterministic advantage is $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +29.60$ points.\nExcluding death episodes, the gap collapses to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$; 75 of 95 non-tie labels (78.9\\%) arise from death asymmetry---a penalty-independent measure.\nThe penalty-sensitive Death Avoidance Factor (DAF; \\S\\ref{sec:daf}) is 90.41\\% at the default penalty $P{=}100$, ranging from 46\\% ($P{=}10$) to 95\\% ($P{=}200$).\nThe per-task correlation between death-rate differential and performance gap is $r = 0.97$ ($p < 10^{-6}$, $n = 11$), confirming that death avoidance is the dominant mechanism across task types (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}).\n\nTwo cross-context checks probe the specificity of this finding.\nFirst, a MATH Level~4--5 evaluation (300 problems), using a code interpreter as the deterministic modality, shows a directional reversal: internal reasoning outperforms code interpreter by $+4.3$ percentage points---the opposite direction from ScienceWorld.\nIn the absence of death risk, the deterministic advantage vanishes.\nSecond, a preliminary cross-model pilot with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ($N{=}10$ episode pairs, 2 task types) shows directionally consistent death patterns within ScienceWorld (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}), suggesting the phenomenon is not specific to a single model, though full-scale multi-model validation remains future work.\nA trained router (Qwen3-1.7B with LoRA) achieves balanced accuracy of only 57.6\\%, barely above chance, consistent with a signal so dominated by death avoidance that the residual learnable structure is thin (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Routing signal decomposition.}\n    We identify step-level oracle degeneracy in sparse-reward environments and introduce episode-level counterfactual analysis with the DAF metric, a methodology for quantifying what fraction of routing signal derives from catastrophic-failure avoidance (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:daf}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Death avoidance dominance.}\n    On ScienceWorld, death asymmetry drives 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels independent of penalty magnitude (DAF = 90.41\\% at $P{=}100$; $r = 0.97$ across task types), and a trained router does not significantly outperform always-deterministic (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-environment negative contrast.}\n    On MATH ($N{=}300$), where death risk is absent, the routing signal reverses direction, providing evidence that the ScienceWorld pattern is driven by its failure structure rather than a universal property of routing (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-model directional evidence.}\n    A Llama-3.1-8B pilot shows consistent death-pattern direction, providing preliminary evidence that the finding is not model-specific (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Router training as diagnostic probe.}\n    Near-random balanced accuracy (57.6\\%) of a trained router confirms that the learnable signal beyond death avoidance is structurally thin (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nThe remainder of the paper is organized as follows.\n\\S\\ref{sec:method} describes the counterfactual collection procedure, episode-level labeling, and the DAF metric.\n\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp} presents the death avoidance decomposition and correlation analysis.\n\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}--\\ref{sec:cross-model} report baselines, router diagnostics, and cross-model verification.\n\\S\\ref{sec:discussion} discusses cross-environment evidence and implications.\n",
      "numLines": 56,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 56
    }
  },

        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \\emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.\nA Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct agent navigating ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}---a text-based science simulation with 30 task types---faces this choice at every step. Tracking temperature thresholds, container states, and multi-step procedures via internal reasoning is error-prone: the model may hallucinate that water has boiled when the stove was never activated, or that an object has been moved when the action was invalid. Deterministic external computation (environment simulators, code interpreters) eliminates such hallucinations via exact state transitions. The practical question for agent design is therefore: \\emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus reasoning internally?}\n\nExisting routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision.\nToolformer \\cite{toolformer-2024} teaches models to insert API calls when doing so reduces perplexity; ART \\cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2024} selects among heterogeneous modules (search, code, vision) per subtask.\nMore recent adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \\cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} or model tiers \\cite{mtrouter-2026}.\nThe shared assumption is that routing signal stems from diverse sources---accuracy, latency, capability matching---and that a learned router can exploit this signal to improve over any fixed strategy.\nBut this assumption has never been systematically verified: \\emph{what actually drives the value of routing decisions in interactive environments?}\n\nWe find a surprisingly concentrated answer: \\textbf{death avoidance accounts for approximately 90\\% of the routing signal} in ScienceWorld.\nThe internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death (a $-100$ penalty), while the deterministic strategy survives the same episodes.\nOnce death episodes are removed, the performance gap between strategies nearly vanishes.\n\nReaching this conclusion requires a methodological contribution.\nThe standard approach to generating routing labels---per-step oracle comparison---degenerates under sparse rewards.\nIn ScienceWorld, non-zero reward arrives only at episode termination; 97.4\\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical scores regardless of modality, leaving no step-level signal to learn from (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}).\nKeyword-based heuristics also fail structurally, as ScienceWorld actions lack science-specific terminology (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}).\nWe instead propose \\emph{episode-level counterfactual analysis}: run complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, then compare episode outcomes to generate routing labels (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}).\n\nAcross 11 ScienceWorld task types (245 episode pairs), the full deterministic advantage is $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +29.60$ points.\nExcluding death episodes, the gap collapses to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$; 75 of 95 non-tie labels (78.9\\%) arise from death asymmetry---a penalty-independent measure.\nThe penalty-sensitive Death Avoidance Factor (DAF; \\S\\ref{sec:daf}) is 90.41\\% at the default penalty $P{=}100$, ranging from 46\\% ($P{=}10$) to 95\\% ($P{=}200$).\nThe per-task correlation between death-rate differential and performance gap is $r = 0.97$ ($p < 10^{-6}$, $n = 11$), confirming that death avoidance is the dominant mechanism across task types (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}).\n\nTwo cross-context checks probe the specificity of this finding.\nFirst, a MATH Level~4--5 evaluation (300 problems), using a code interpreter as the deterministic modality, shows a directional reversal: internal reasoning outperforms code interpreter by $+4.3$ percentage points---the opposite direction from ScienceWorld.\nIn the absence of death risk, the deterministic advantage vanishes.\nSecond, a preliminary cross-model pilot with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ($N{=}10$ episode pairs, 2 task types) shows directionally consistent death patterns within ScienceWorld (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}), suggesting the phenomenon is not specific to a single model, though full-scale multi-model validation remains future work.\nA trained router (Qwen3-1.7B with LoRA) achieves balanced accuracy of only 57.6\\%, barely above chance, consistent with a signal so dominated by death avoidance that the residual learnable structure is thin (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Routing signal decomposition.}\n    We identify step-level oracle degeneracy in sparse-reward environments and introduce episode-level counterfactual analysis with the DAF metric, a methodology for quantifying what fraction of routing signal derives from catastrophic-failure avoidance (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:daf}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Death avoidance dominance.}\n    On ScienceWorld, death asymmetry drives 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels independent of penalty magnitude (DAF = 90.41\\% at $P{=}100$; $r = 0.97$ across task types), and a trained router does not significantly outperform always-deterministic (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-environment negative contrast.}\n    On MATH ($N{=}300$), where death risk is absent, the routing signal reverses direction, providing evidence that the ScienceWorld pattern is driven by its failure structure rather than a universal property of routing (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-model directional evidence.}\n    A Llama-3.1-8B pilot shows consistent death-pattern direction, providing preliminary evidence that the finding is not model-specific (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Router training as diagnostic probe.}\n    Near-random balanced accuracy (57.6\\%) of a trained router confirms that the learnable signal beyond death avoidance is structurally thin (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nThe remainder of the paper is organized as follows.\n\\S\\ref{sec:method} describes the counterfactual collection procedure, episode-level labeling, and the DAF metric.\n\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp} presents the death avoidance decomposition and correlation analysis.\n\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}--\\ref{sec:cross-model} report baselines, router diagnostics, and cross-model verification.\n\\S\\ref{sec:discussion} discusses cross-environment evidence and implications.\n",

        "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-environment evidence.}\nThe death-avoidance finding in ScienceWorld raises a natural question: does routing signal persist in environments without catastrophic failure? An evaluation on MATH Level~4--5 (300 problems: 152 Level~4, 148 Level~5), using code interpreter as the deterministic modality, suggests not. Internal reasoning directionally outperforms code interpreter: 81.0\\% accuracy (95\\% CI [76.7, 85.3]) versus 76.7\\% (95\\% CI [72.0, 81.3]); the paired difference of $+4.3$~percentage points (95\\% CI [0.0, $+8.7$]; McNemar $p = 0.079$) shows a directional reversal, though not statistically significant at the 95\\% level. The label distribution inverts relative to ScienceWorld: 30 internal-preferred versus 17 deterministic-preferred (compared to 9:86 in ScienceWorld), with 84.3\\% of problems (253 of 300) showing no modality preference. In the absence of death risk, the routing signal vanishes or reverses---consistent with death avoidance as the primary driver of the deterministic strategy's advantage observed in ScienceWorld.\n\n\\paragraph{Implications for router design.}\nThe dominance of death avoidance in routing signal (DAF exceeding 90\\%) reframes what a modality router is learning. Rather than discovering efficiency-quality tradeoffs across reasoning strategies, the router primarily learns to predict which states lead to catastrophic failure under internal reasoning. This aligns the routing problem more closely with \\emph{safety critics} in safe reinforcement learning~\\citep{garcia2015comprehensive} than with the cost-quality optimization framing adopted by existing routing work~\\citep{cogrouter-2026, ares-2026, mtrouter-2026}. The practical implication is direct: if death avoidance accounts for the vast majority of routing value, then a lightweight death predictor---trained on state features correlated with hallucination-induced failure---may capture most of the router's benefit without the overhead of full counterfactual labeling. That the oracle outperforms always-deterministic by only 2.89 points further suggests that the residual, non-death routing signal may not justify the complexity of learning a full routing policy.\n\n\\paragraph{Label imbalance as a core finding.}\nThe extreme label distribution---86 deterministic-preferred, 9 internal-preferred, and 150 no-preference episodes---is not an artifact to be corrected but a central result. The 61.2\\% no-preference rate indicates that for most episodes, modality choice is irrelevant: both strategies succeed or both fail. Among episodes where modality matters, deterministic is preferred at a 9.6:1 ratio, driven almost entirely by death asymmetry. This explains why always-deterministic is a strong baseline: the router's marginal value is confined to identifying the ${\\sim}$4\\% of episodes where internal reasoning is genuinely superior. The near-random balanced accuracy of trained routers (57--59\\%) is a downstream consequence: with so few positive examples for the minority class, supervised learning cannot reliably distinguish rare internal-preferred episodes from noise. Recent concurrent work on tool-use calibration reaches compatible conclusions from the opposite direction---that LLMs may already possess implicit routing signals~\\citep{when2tool-2026} but that external tool invocation carries non-trivial costs~\\citep{tool-use-tax-2026}, reinforcing the case for conservative routing defaults.\n\n\\paragraph{Penalty sensitivity and generalization.}\nThe DAF's sensitivity to penalty magnitude---ranging from 46\\% at $P{=}10$ to 95\\% at $P{=}200$---highlights that death-avoidance dominance is not an invariant property of routing but a function of the environment's penalty structure. In domains with mild failure costs, non-death routing signals (e.g., reasoning quality differences on surviving episodes) would constitute a larger share of total routing value. This connects to reward shaping more broadly: the relative magnitude of catastrophic versus incremental penalties shapes which behavioral differences a router can exploit.\n\nOur findings may also be capability-dependent. The 42.9\\% internal-reasoning death rate reflects the failure mode of a 7B-parameter model prone to state hallucination. Larger models with lower hallucination rates would exhibit smaller death-rate differentials, potentially reducing DAF and revealing routing signal currently masked by catastrophic failures. Whether non-death routing signal \\emph{emerges} as model capability increases---or remains negligible---is an open question with direct implications for the modality routing agenda.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope and future directions.}\nOur analysis is grounded in a single interactive environment with a specific failure mode (the $-100$ death penalty). Extending the counterfactual methodology to environments with graded failure costs, embodied agents with continuous action spaces, or multi-turn dialogue systems would clarify how broadly death avoidance---or analogous catastrophic-failure avoidance---accounts for apparent routing signal. Step-level counterfactual labeling, rather than episode-level, is a complementary direction that could reveal finer-grained routing structure masked by our current aggregation. A further limitation is the gap between the implicit routing signal we measure---based on episode outcomes---and the logit-level representations an implicit router would use at inference time; bridging this gap requires probing whether outcome-level modality preferences are recoverable from the model's internal activations before action selection. Finally, validating these findings across model scales and families beyond 7--8B parameters would establish whether the death-avoidance dominance we observe is a property of the environment or of the capability regime.\n",
        "numLines": 20,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 20
      }

  "parentUuid": "c96ac785-ee20-468f-acf9-961aacecbf83",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Tool Use and External Computation in LLMs.}\nA growing body of work augments language models with external tools.\nToolformer \\cite{toolformer-2024} teaches LLMs to insert API calls via self-supervised loss filtering, while ToolkenGPT \\cite{toolkengpt-2023} represents tools as special tokens in the embedding space, enabling frozen models to invoke massive tool libraries.\nART \\cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into multi-step programs that interleave reasoning with tool calls, and Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2024} composes heterogeneous modules (search, code, vision) via LLM-generated programs.\nReTool \\cite{retool-2026} applies reinforcement learning to let models discover when code execution is preferable to chain-of-thought.\nThese approaches focus on \\emph{learning to use tools}; our work instead asks a prior question: \\emph{what signal drives the decision to route to an external modality at all?}\n\nRecent concurrent studies sharpen this question from multiple angles.\n\\citet{tool-overuse-illusion-2026} identify a cognitive-illusion effect in which LLMs default to external tools even when internal knowledge suffices.\n\\citet{when2tool-2026} show that tool-call decisions are already encoded in hidden states before explicit reasoning occurs.\n\\citet{tool-use-tax-2026} quantify a ``tool-use tax''---cases where invoking a tool degrades rather than improves performance.\n\\citet{to-call-or-not-2026} propose a necessity--utility--affordability framework for deciding whether to call a tool.\nOur counterfactual decomposition provides complementary, environment-grounded evidence: in ScienceWorld, ${\\sim}90\\%$ of the routing signal at $P{=}100$ traces to catastrophic-failure prevention rather than general utility, offering a concrete quantification that these frameworks currently lack.\n\n\\paragraph{Adaptive Computation and Routing.}\nRather than adding new tools, a parallel line of research routes inputs to different computation budgets or models.\nCogRouter \\cite{cogrouter-2026} adapts reasoning depth per step, ARES \\cite{ares-2026} selects among reasoning effort levels for LLM agents, and Mixture-of-Recursions \\cite{mixture-of-recursions-2025} learns token-level recursion depths within a single model.\nMTRouter \\cite{mtrouter-2026} addresses multi-turn routing across heterogeneous models by jointly embedding conversation history and model capabilities.\nThese systems optimize \\emph{how} to route; we focus on \\emph{why} routing helps.\nOur counterfactual analysis reveals that, in interactive environments, the dominant routing signal is not efficiency or capability matching but catastrophic-failure avoidance---a finding that could inform the objective design of future routers.\n\n\\paragraph{Counterfactual Evaluation in RL.}\nCounterfactual reasoning has been used to explain RL agent behavior post hoc.\n\\citet{olson2021counterfactual} generate counterfactual states via deep generative models to highlight features critical to an agent's decisions.\n\\citet{gajcin2023counterfactual} produce counterfactual action explanations that show users which alternative actions would have led to different outcomes.\n\\citet{amitai2024explaining} extend this to action-outcome explanations, contrasting observed and hypothetical trajectories at the step level.\nA common thread is that these methods perturb \\emph{individual actions} within a fixed policy to attribute credit.\nOur approach differs in both purpose and granularity: we execute two \\emph{complete episodes} under different routing strategies (LLM-only vs.\\ LLM+simulator), producing prospective paired comparisons that isolate the routing signal \\emph{before} any router is trained.\nThis episode-level design sidesteps compounding perturbation artifacts and directly measures the aggregate effect of modality access on task outcomes.\n\n\\paragraph{Safe RL and Catastrophic Failure.}\n\\citet{garcia2015comprehensive} survey safe RL methods that constrain policies to avoid catastrophic outcomes, categorizing approaches into constrained optimization, risk-sensitive criteria, and external-knowledge incorporation.\nA common mechanism is reward shaping with large negative penalties for unsafe states, which implicitly teaches agents to avoid irreversible failures.\nOur findings connect to this literature from an analytical rather than algorithmic perspective: the counterfactual decomposition reveals that death avoidance---preventing irreversible task failure---is the dominant component of the routing signal in ScienceWorld.\nThis suggests that modality routing in interactive environments functions primarily as a safety mechanism, and that router design may benefit from explicitly modeling catastrophic-failure risk rather than treating routing as a generic capability-matching problem.\n\n\\paragraph{LLM Agents in Interactive Environments.}\nScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022} provides a text-based science-experiment environment requiring multi-step causal reasoning, serving as our primary testbed.\nALFWorld \\cite{alfworld-2021} offers a complementary text-based household environment; Voyager \\cite{voyager-2023} demonstrates open-ended exploration in Minecraft using LLM-driven curriculum and skill libraries.\nReAct \\cite{react-2023} interleaves reasoning traces with environment actions and serves as our internal-reasoning baseline.\nMore recent work augments agents with world models: ITP \\cite{itp-2026} uses learned world models for lookahead planning, while Dyna-Think \\cite{dynathink-2025} synergizes reasoning, acting, and world-model simulation.\nBoth inherit the hallucination risks of approximate world models.\nOur deterministic simulator modality provides exact state transitions, and our analysis shows that this exactness matters almost exclusively for death avoidance---the cases where hallucinated transitions would be most costly.\n",
        "numLines": 46,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 46
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/related_work.tex"
  },

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "814809cc-c882-4e11-9aff-47c9605c9a91",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/method.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment $\\mathcal{E}$ with state space $\\mathcal{S}$, observation space $\\mathcal{O}$, and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent follows a policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ that maps observation histories to actions. At each step $t$, the agent receives observation $o_t$, selects action $a_t = \\pi(o_1, \\ldots, o_t)$, and the environment transitions to state $s_{t+1}$.\n\nWe define a modality set $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$ governing how the agent processes state transitions:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Internal} ($m = {\\tt int}$): The agent uses its own generation to predict intermediate observations. The LLM interleaves chain-of-thought reasoning with environment actions following the ReAct paradigm \\cite{react-2023}, but prediction errors compound across steps.\n    \\item \\textbf{Deterministic} ($m = {\\tt det}$): The agent receives ground-truth observations from the environment simulator at each step---equivalent to access to a perfect dynamics model. Action selection remains with the LLM; only the state-transition prediction is replaced.\n\\end{itemize}\n\nA \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which modality to use at each step. The goal of modality routing is to learn $\\rho$ that maximizes expected episode return. Our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn such a router.\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling assigns routing labels by comparing single-step outcomes under each computation modality. Under sparse terminal rewards, this approach degenerates: on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical intermediate scores regardless of modality, yielding no routing signal. We instead perform episode-level counterfactual analysis.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and modality $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying modality $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), ensuring that performance differences arise solely from the modality.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ is the death penalty ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair thus produces a tuple:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}), s(\\tau_{\\tt det}), d(\\tau_{\\tt int}), d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{equation}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct (vLLM) on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type).\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{equation}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow\\; \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{equation}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, reflecting the asymmetric cost of catastrophic failure in interactive environments. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. The full procedure is given in Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}.\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}\n\\label{alg:labeling}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\REQUIRE Task instance $t$, modalities $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$\n\\ENSURE Label $\\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}$\n\\FOR{each $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$}\n    \\STATE Run episode $\\tau_m$ with pure-strategy $m$\n    \\STATE Record score $s_m$ and death indicator $d_m$\n\\ENDFOR\n\\IF{$d_{\\tt int} = 1$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 0$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = 0$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{both dead}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt det} > s_{\\tt int}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt int} > s_{\\tt det}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSE\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{tied alive}\n\\ENDIF\n\\RETURN $\\ell$\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\end{algorithm}\n\nThe four mutually exclusive cases correspond to:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Death asymmetry} (lines 5--8): $d^{\\tt int} \\neq d^{\\tt det}$ $\\rightarrow$ the surviving strategy is preferred. This accounts for 78.9\\% of all non-tie labels.\n    \\item \\textbf{Both dead} (lines 9--10): $d^{\\tt int} = d^{\\tt det} = 1$ $\\rightarrow$ ${\\tt no\\_preference}$.\n    \\item \\textbf{Score comparison} (lines 11--14): both survive and $s^{\\tt det} \\neq s^{\\tt int}$ $\\rightarrow$ the higher-scoring strategy is preferred.\n    \\item \\textbf{Tied alive} (lines 15--16): both survive and $s^{\\tt det} = s^{\\tt int}$ $\\rightarrow$ ${\\tt no\\_preference}$.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nLabel distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. The full score gap decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation holds exactly when the surviving strategy scores zero in all death-asymmetry episodes; in our data, the residual is small ($29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$, or $0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1, indicating death avoidance overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nDAF is well-defined when $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} > 0$; in our data, the minimum per-task $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ is $+9.58$ (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing}), ensuring numerical stability. Since DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$---a mathematical consequence, not an empirical finding. We therefore report both DAF at the environment default ($P{=}100$) and the penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction (78.9\\%).\n\nTo assess sensitivity to penalty magnitude, we recalculate DAF under hypothetical death penalties (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}): $P{=}10 \\rightarrow$ DAF $= 46.4\\%$, $P{=}50 \\rightarrow 81.2\\%$, $P{=}100 \\rightarrow 89.6\\%$, $P{=}200 \\rightarrow 94.5\\%$. Death avoidance remains the dominant component across all penalty magnitudes, though its relative contribution scales with penalty severity. As a penalty-independent companion metric, 75 of 95 non-tie routing labels (78.9\\%) are driven by death asymmetry rather than score comparison (\\S\\ref{sec:labeling}), confirming that death avoidance dominates routing signal regardless of penalty magnitude.\n",
        "numLines": 101,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 101
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/method.tex"
  },

### Tool result

_source line 711 · 2026-05-15T10:24:32.005Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01TysAHooATLFssMpL6TAyif`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Modular LLM agents that route between neural reasoning and deterministic computation promise improved reliability in rule-governed environments, yet understanding \emph{what} drives effective routing remains unclear. Per-step oracle labeling degenerates in sparse-reward settings---in ScienceWorld, 97.4\% of steps yield tied outcomes, rendering step-level analysis uninformative. We propose episode-level counterfactual analysis: running complete episodes under pure strategies and comparing final outcomes to derive per-episode routing labels. Across 245 episodes with a Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct backbone, death asymmetry drives 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels independent of penalty magnitude (DAF ranges 46--95\% across $P{\in}[10{,}200]$, reaching 90\% at the default $P{=}100$), with strong positive cross-task correlation ($r = 0.97$, $n = 11$). These results indicate that deterministic computation's value is concentrated in catastrophic failure prevention rather than positive task guidance.
3	\end{abstract}
```

</details>

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We presented episode-level counterfactual analysis for diagnosing routing in modular LLM agents. On 245 ScienceWorld episodes (11 task types), death asymmetry drives 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels independent of penalty magnitude (DAF = 90.41\% at $P{=}100$, range 46--95\%), with strong cross-task correlation ($r = 0.97$), oracle degeneracy (96.3\% always-deterministic), and near-random router accuracy ($58.1 \pm 0.8\%$). Preliminary MATH and ALFWorld studies provide consistent cross-environment evidence, though mechanisms differ (death avoidance vs.\ action space pruning).
```

1	\documentclass[11pt]{article}
2	
3	\usepackage[review]{acl}
4	
5	\usepackage{times}

14	\usepackage{algorithmic}
15	
16	\title{Death Avoidance as the Dominant Routing Signal:\\A Counterfactual Analysis on ScienceWorld}
17	
18	\author{Anonymous}
19	
20	\begin{document}
21	\maketitle
22	
23	\input{abstract}
24	\input{introduction}
25	\input{method}
26	\input{experiments}
27	\input{related_work}
28	\input{conclusion}
29	
30	\section*{Limitations}
31	
32	Our analysis uses a single benchmark (ScienceWorld) with a single LLM backbone (Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), and generalization to other interactive environments and model scales remains to be established. A preliminary cross-model pilot with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ($N{=}10$ episode pairs, 2 task types) shows directionally consistent death patterns (\S\ref{sec:cross-model}), but full-scale multi-model validation remains future work. The $-100$ death penalty may amplify DAF; environments with less catastrophic penalties could exhibit lower DAF, though the qualitative finding may still hold. On the 135 episodes (55.1\%) where neither strategy leads to catastrophic failure, deterministic computation retains a small advantage ($\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$), indicating non-zero routing signal beyond death avoidance. Our analysis covers 11 of 30 ScienceWorld task types; the remaining 19 (63\%) were excluded during prescreening because both strategies timed out on all seed variations within the 30-step budget, providing no counterfactual signal. These tasks likely require longer-horizon planning; increasing the step budget could surface additional routing signal whose composition may differ from the death-dominated pattern observed here. Stronger models (e.g., 70B-scale or reasoning-specialized) likely exhibit lower internal-strategy death rates, which would reduce DAF. Our finding may reflect a capability-dependent failure mode: at higher capability levels, death avoidance may no longer dominate routing signal.
33	
34	\paragraph{Cross-environment evidence.}
35	A MATH Level~4--5 evaluation (300 problems: 152 Level~4, 148 Level~5), using code interpreter as the deterministic modality, provides evidence beyond ScienceWorld. Internal reasoning directionally outperforms code interpreter: 81.0\% accuracy (95\% CI [76.7, 85.3]) versus 76.7\% (95\% CI [72.0, 81.3])---a reversal of $+4.3$~percentage points (95\% CI [0.0, $+8.7$]; McNemar $p = 0.079$). More strikingly, the label distribution inverts relative to ScienceWorld: 30 internal-preferred versus 17 deterministic-preferred (compared to 9:86 in ScienceWorld), while 84.3\% of problems (253 of 300) show no modality preference. In the absence of death risk, the deterministic strategy's advantage vanishes---consistent with death avoidance as the primary driver of the routing signal observed in ScienceWorld. An exploratory ALFWorld study \cite{alfworld-2021} (135 games, 6 task types) shows a related but mechanistically distinct pattern: 25.9\% of episodes are deterministic-preferred versus 1.5\% internal-preferred, with deterministic success rate (27.4\%) far exceeding internal (3.0\%). However, the underlying mechanism differs from ScienceWorld. In ScienceWorld, the internal strategy has baseline competence (57.1\% survival rate) but suffers hallucination-induced catastrophic failures; in ALFWorld, the 3\% internal success rate reflects a fundamental capability gap---without admissible action filtering, the agent rarely completes tasks at all. The deterministic advantage in ALFWorld thus stems from action space pruning rather than death avoidance per se. These results are suggestive but do not directly confirm that the DAF pattern generalizes; environments with intermediate failure modes remain to be studied.
36	
37	\paragraph{Step-level mislabeling.} Episode-level labels may assign suboptimal routing decisions to individual steps; in particular, steps preceding a death event are labeled det-preferred even if the death was not foreseeable from those steps' observations. Training on pure-strategy trajectories but deploying on mixed policy also introduces a distribution shift \citep{dagger-2011}.
38	
39	\paragraph{Scope.}
40	Extending the counterfactual methodology to environments with graded failure costs, embodied agents, or multi-turn dialogue systems would clarify how broadly death avoidance accounts for apparent routing signal. Step-level labeling with denser rewards and the logit--generation gap in the implicit router are natural next steps. We audit labeling quality in Appendix~\ref{app:labeling}.
41	
42	\section*{Ethics Statement}
43	
44	This work uses ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022}, a publicly available text-based simulation environment, and does not involve human subjects or private data. All language models are accessed via standard APIs or publicly released weights. We report computational costs: approximately 40 GPU-hours on two RTX 5090 GPUs for data collection, router training, and evaluation.
45	
46	\bibliography{references}
47	
48	\appendix
49	
50	\section{Labeling Quality Audit}
51	\label{app:labeling}
52	
53	A potential concern is oracle circularity: whether episode-level labels reflect tie-breaking artifacts rather than genuine modality differences. We audit all 245 labels. Of the 150 \texttt{no\_preference} labels (61.2\%), 115 (46.9\%) are both-alive ties and 35 (14.3\%) are both-dead---all excluded from router training. The remaining 95 training labels are entirely genuine: 75 from death asymmetry (70 deterministic-preferred, 5 internal-preferred) and 20 from score comparison with both strategies surviving (16 deterministic-preferred, 4 internal-preferred). Zero labels depend on tie-breaking. Retraining the router using only death-asymmetry labels yields balanced accuracy of 57.3\%, within 1$\sigma$ of the full-label retrain (57.6 $\pm$ 0.5\%), confirming that score-comparison labels contribute negligible additional signal. The 97.4\% step-level tie rate (\S\ref{sec:collection}) further motivates episode-level over step-level labeling.
54	
55	\section{Per-Task Decomposition}
56	\label{app:per-task}
57	We select 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types via prescreening: task types where both strategies complete at least one episode within the 30-step budget are retained; types where both strategies time out on all seed variations are excluded.\footnote{Prescreening ensures that the counterfactual comparison is meaningful---task types where neither strategy makes progress provide no routing signal.}

87	\section{Correlation Robustness}
88	\label{app:correlation}
89	
90	The Pearson $r = 0.9698$ reported in \S\ref{sec:death-decomp} is robust across multiple diagnostics. Spearman rank correlation is $\rho = 0.986$ ($p = 2.4 \times 10^{-8}$), confirming that the relationship is not driven by outliers or non-linearity. A nonparametric bootstrap (10{,}000 resamples) yields 95\% CI $[0.952, 0.995]$.

116	\section{Router Training Details}
117	\label{app:router-details}
118	
119	We use Qwen3-1.7B for the router to avoid circular dependency with the Qwen2.5-7B data-generating model.
120	
121	Gradient accumulation is critical for the implicit router: with gradient accumulation of 1, the model fails to achieve meaningful routing discrimination (internal recall 26.7\%); with gradient accumulation of 8, the model becomes non-degenerate (internal recall above 50\% across all seeds). All main-text results use gradient accumulation 8.
122	
123	Cross-seed balanced accuracy is stable (57--59\%), but internal recall varies from 51.5\% to 81.8\%, reflecting the structurally thin learnable signal: when ${\sim}90\%$ of routing value is death avoidance, the non-death signal is inherently noisy. Deployed end-to-end, the trained router allocates 97.0\% of episodes to deterministic computation with 17.6\% death rate, and misrouting cost (death from internally-routed episodes) exceeds the benefit of correctly routing rare internal-preferred cases.

A preliminary cross-model pilot with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ($N{=}10$ episode pairs, 2 task types) shows directionally consistent death patterns (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}), but full-scale multi-model validation remains future work. The $-100$ death penalty may amplify DAF; environments with less catastrophic penalties could exhibit lower DAF, though the qualitative finding may still hold. On the 135 episodes (55.1\\%) where neither strategy leads to catastrophic failure, deterministic computation retains a small advantage ($\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$), indicating non-zero routing signal beyond death avoidance. Our analysis covers 11 of 30 ScienceWorld task types; the remaining 19 (63\\%) were excluded during prescreening because both strategies timed out on all seed variations within the 30-step budget, providing no counterfactual signal. These tasks likely require longer-horizon planning; increasing the step budget could surface additional routing signal whose composition may differ from the death-dominated pattern observed here. Stronger models (e.g., 70B-scale or reasoning-specialized) likely exhibit lower internal-strategy death rates, which would reduce DAF. Our finding may reflect a capability-dependent failure mode: at higher capability levels, death avoidance may no longer dominate routing signal.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-environment evidence.}\nA MATH Level~4--5 evaluation (300 problems: 152 Level~4, 148 Level~5), using code interpreter as the deterministic modality, provides evidence beyond ScienceWorld. Internal reasoning directionally outperforms code interpreter: 81.0\\% accuracy (95\\% CI [76.7, 85.3]) versus 76.7\\% (95\\% CI [72.0, 81.3])---a reversal of $+4.3$~percentage points (95\\% CI [0.0, $+8.7$]; McNemar $p = 0.079$). More strikingly, the label distribution inverts relative to ScienceWorld: 30 internal-preferred versus 17 deterministic-preferred (compared to 9:86 in ScienceWorld), while 84.3\\% of problems (253 of 300) show no modality preference. In the absence of death risk, the deterministic strategy's advantage vanishes---consistent with death avoidance as the primary driver of the routing signal observed in ScienceWorld. An exploratory ALFWorld study \\cite{alfworld-2021} (135 games, 6 task types) shows a related but mechanistically distinct pattern: 25.9\\% of episodes are deterministic-preferred versus 1.5\\% internal-preferred, with deterministic success rate (27.4\\%) far exceeding internal (3.0\\%). However, the underlying mechanism differs from ScienceWorld. In ScienceWorld, the internal strategy has baseline competence (57.1\\% survival rate) but suffers hallucination-induced catastrophic failures; in ALFWorld, the 3\\% internal success rate reflects a fundamental capability gap---without admissible action filtering, the agent rarely completes tasks at all. The deterministic advantage in ALFWorld thus stems from action space pruning rather than death avoidance per se. These results are suggestive but do not directly confirm that the DAF pattern generalizes; environments with intermediate failure modes remain to be studied.\n\n\\paragraph{Step-level mislabeling.} Episode-level labels may assign suboptimal routing decisions to individual steps; in particular, steps preceding a death event are labeled det-preferred even if the death was not foreseeable from those steps' observations. Training on pure-strategy trajectories but deploying on mixed policy also introduces a distribution shift \\citep{dagger-2011}.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope.}\nExtending the counterfactual methodology to environments with graded failure costs, embodied agents, or multi-turn dialogue systems would clarify how broadly death avoidance accounts for apparent routing signal. Step-level labeling with denser rewards and the logit--generation gap in the implicit router are natural next steps. We audit labeling quality in Appendix~\\ref{app:labeling}.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a publicly available text-based simulation environment, and does not involve human subjects or private data. All language models are accessed via standard APIs or publicly released weights. We report computational costs: approximately 40 GPU-hours on two RTX 5090 GPUs for data collection, router training, and evaluation.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\n\\section{Labeling Quality Audit}\n\\label{app:labeling}\n\nA potential concern is oracle circularity: whether episode-level labels reflect tie-breaking artifacts rather than genuine modality differences. We audit all 245 labels. Of the 150 \\texttt{no\\_preference} labels (61.2\\%), 115 (46.9\\%) are both-alive ties and 35 (14.3\\%) are both-dead---all excluded from router training. The remaining 95 training labels are entirely genuine: 75 from death asymmetry (70 deterministic-preferred, 5 internal-preferred) and 20 from score comparison with both strategies surviving (16 deterministic-preferred, 4 internal-preferred). Zero labels depend on tie-breaking. Retraining the router using only death-asymmetry labels yields balanced accuracy of 57.3\\%, within 1$\\sigma$ of the full-label retrain (57.6 $\\pm$ 0.5\\%), confirming that score-comparison labels contribute negligible additional signal. <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01L77hUxaR5zhK2V4fhGMvY5`</summary>

```text
67:To test whether the death avoidance pattern is model-specific, we run a mechanism verification pilot with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on two task types (\texttt{freeze} and \texttt{find-non-living-thing}, 5 episode pairs each, $N{=}10$). Llama-8B achieves lower absolute ScienceWorld scores than Qwen-2.5-7B, reflecting weaker task-level capability, but the death pattern direction is consistent across model families.
89:Table~\ref{tab:cross-model} summarizes the comparison. This pilot ($N{=}10$ episode pairs) provides preliminary directional evidence only: the sample size precludes statistical claims. Nevertheless, observing the same death asymmetry direction in a different model family (Llama vs.\ Qwen) suggests that the pattern reflects a structural property of internal reasoning in interactive environments rather than a model-specific artifact.
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KB4U3Hy262GJ9tHZwag6LM`</summary>

```text
88:    \Delta_{\text{full}} \approx \underbrace{\delta \cdot P}_{\text{death contribution}} + \Delta_{\text{nd}}
94:    \text{DAF} = 1 - \frac{\Delta_{\text{nd}}}{\Delta_{\text{full}}} \approx \frac{\delta P}{\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}}}
98:DAF is well-defined when $\Delta_{\text{full}} > 0$; in our data, the minimum per-task $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ is $+9.58$ (\texttt{find-non-living-thing}), ensuring numerical stability. Since DAF $= \delta P / (\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$---a mathematical consequence, not an empirical finding. We therefore report both DAF at the environment default ($P{=}100$) and the penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction (78.9\%).
```

Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). 31	\section*{Limitations}
32	
33	Our analysis uses a single benchmark (ScienceWorld) with a single LLM backbone (Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), and generalization to other interactive environments and model scales remains to be established. A preliminary cross-model pilot with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ($N{=}10$ episode pairs, 2 task types) shows directionally consistent death patterns (\S\ref{sec:cross-model}), but full-scale multi-model validation remains future work. The $-100$ death penalty may amplify DAF; environments with less catastrophic penalties could exhibit lower DAF, though the qualitative finding may still hold. On the 135 episodes (55.1\%) where neither strategy leads to catastrophic failure, deterministic computation retains a small advantage ($\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$), indicating non-zero routing signal beyond death avoidance. Our analysis covers 11 of 30 ScienceWorld task types; the remaining 19 (63\%) were excluded during prescreening because both strategies timed out on all seed variations within the 30-step budget, providing no counterfactual signal. These tasks likely require longer-horizon planning; increasing the step budget could surface additional routing signal whose composition may differ from the death-dominated pattern observed here. Stronger models (e.g., 70B-scale or reasoning-specialized) likely exhibit lower internal-strategy death rates, which would reduce DAF. Our finding may reflect a capability-dependent failure mode: at higher capability levels, death avoidance may no longer dominate routing signal.
34	
35	\paragraph{Cross-environment scope.}
```

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Modular LLM agents that route between neural reasoning and deterministic computation promise improved reliability, yet understanding \emph{what} drives the value of routing decisions remains unclear. We propose episode-level counterfactual analysis: running complete episodes under pure strategies and comparing final outcomes to decompose routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components. Across 245 ScienceWorld episodes (11 task types, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), death avoidance accounts for approximately 90\% of routing signal (DAF $= 90.41\%$ at $P{=}100$; Pearson $r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$), with 78.9\% of non-tie labels arising from death asymmetry alone. A trained router achieves only 57--59\% balanced accuracy, confirming thin learnable signal beyond survival. Cross-environment analysis on MATH ($N{=}300$) shows routing signal vanishes without a death penalty: the label distribution inverts from 86:9 (deterministic-preferred) to 30:17 (internal directionally favored), with a non-significant reversal of $+4.3$ percentage points. A Llama-3.1-8B pilot provides preliminary directional evidence that death-driven routing generalizes across model families. These findings indicate that in environments with catastrophic failure modes, routing value is dominated by safety---the router functions primarily as a safety critic rather than an efficiency optimizer.
3	\end{abstract}

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01SkRSkyodgk4oxKpW1iWZnu`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We introduced episode-level counterfactual analysis as a methodology for decomposing the routing signal in modular LLM agents into death-avoidance and non-death components. Applied to 245 ScienceWorld episodes across 11 task types with a Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct backbone, this analysis reveals that death avoidance accounts for approximately 90\% of routing value (DAF $= 90.41\%$ at $P{=}100$; $\Delta_\text{full} = 29.60$ vs.\ $\Delta_\text{nd} = 2.84$), with 78.9\% of non-tie labels arising from death asymmetry alone and strong cross-task correlation ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$).
4	
5	Two lines of evidence support the generality of this finding. First, cross-environment analysis on MATH ($N{=}300$), where no death penalty exists, shows that the routing signal vanishes and directionally reverses: internal reasoning scores $+4.3$ percentage points higher than code execution ($81.0\%$ vs.\ $76.7\%$; CI $[0, 8.7]$, not significant), and the label distribution inverts from 86:9 deterministic-preferred in ScienceWorld to 30:17 internal-preferred. This contrast confirms that death avoidance, not an inherent advantage of deterministic computation, is the primary driver. Second, a Llama-3.1-8B pilot replicates the direction of the death differential (freeze death gap $+20\%$, consistent with Qwen's $+30\%$), providing preliminary evidence that the pattern generalizes across model families.
6	
7	A trained routing classifier achieves only 57--59\% balanced accuracy across four seeds, confirming that once death avoidance is accounted for, the remaining learnable signal is thin. The logit-generation gap in the classification head further suggests that discriminative routing over LLM representations is fundamentally limited in this regime.
8	
9	These results carry a practical implication: in environments with catastrophic failure modes, routing value is dominated by safety rather than efficiency. The router functions primarily as a safety critic---steering away from fatal actions---rather than as an optimizer selecting the more efficient strategy. System designers may therefore benefit from treating routing as a risk-management mechanism in such settings.
10	
11	Several directions remain open. Step-level counterfactual labeling could localize where within an episode routing decisions matter most. Stronger backbone models with lower intrinsic death rates may yield lower DAF values, revealing whether non-death routing signal emerges as the safety floor rises. Finally, environments with graded failure costs (rather than binary death) would test whether the dominance of catastrophic-failure avoidance extends to softer penalty structures.
```

```text
35	\paragraph{Cross-environment scope.}
36	Our MATH and ALFWorld analyses (\S\ref{sec:discussion}) provide cross-environment evidence but do not conclusively establish generalization. In MATH (no death penalty), the deterministic advantage vanishes with a directional reversal favoring internal reasoning ($+4.3$\,pp, not statistically significant). In ALFWorld, the deterministic advantage stems from a capability gap rather than death avoidance. Environments with graded failure costs or intermediate penalty regimes remain unstudied.
37	
38	\paragraph{Step-level mislabeling.} Episode-level labels may assign suboptimal routing decisions to individual steps; in particular, steps preceding a death event are labeled det-preferred even if the death was not foreseeable from those steps' observations. Training on pure-strategy trajectories but deploying on mixed policy also introduces a distribution shift \citep{dagger-2011}.
39	
40	\paragraph{Scope.}
41	Extending the counterfactual methodology to environments with graded failure costs, embodied agents, or multi-turn dialogue systems would clarify how broadly death avoidance accounts for apparent routing signal. Step-level labeling with denser rewards and the logit--generation gap in the implicit router are natural next steps. We audit labeling quality in Appendix~\ref{app:labeling}.
42	
43	\section*{Ethics Statement}
44	
45	This work uses ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022}, a publicly available text-based simulation environment, and does not involve human subjects or private data. All language models are accessed via standard APIs or publicly released weights. We report computational costs: approximately 40 GPU-hours on two RTX 5090 GPUs for data collection, router training, and evaluation.
46	
47	\bibliography{references}
48	
49	\appendix
50	
51	\section{Labeling Quality Audit}
52	\label{app:labeling}
53	
54	A potential concern is oracle circularity: whether episode-level labels reflect tie-breaking artifacts rather than genuine modality differences. We audit all 245 labels. Of the 150 \texttt{no\_preference} labels (61.2\%), 115 (46.9\%) are both-alive ties and 35 (14.3\%) are both-dead---all excluded from router training. The remaining 95 training labels are entirely genuine: 75 from death asymmetry (70 deterministic-preferred, 5 internal-preferred) and 20 from score comparison with both strategies surviving (16 deterministic-preferred, 4 internal-preferred). Zero labels depend on tie-breaking. Retraining the router using only death-asymmetry labels yields balanced accuracy of 57.3\%, within 1$\sigma$ of the full-label retrain (57.6 $\pm$ 0.5\%), confirming that score-comparison labels contribute negligible additional signal. The 97.4\% step-level tie rate (\S\ref{sec:collection}) further motivates episode-level over step-level labeling.
55	
56	\section{Per-Task Decomposition}
57	\label{app:per-task}
58	We select 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types via prescreening: task types where both strategies complete at least one episode within the 30-step budget are retained; types where both strategies time out on all seed variations are excluded.\footnote{Prescreening ensures that the counterfactual comparison is meaningful---task types where neither strategy makes progress provide no routing signal.}
59	
60	\begin{table}[h]
61	\centering
62	\scriptsize

88	\section{Correlation Robustness}
89	\label{app:correlation}
90	
91	The Pearson $r = 0.9698$ reported in \S\ref{sec:death-decomp} is robust across multiple diagnostics. Spearman rank correlation is $\rho = 0.986$ ($p = 2.4 \times 10^{-8}$), confirming that the relationship is not driven by outliers or non-linearity. A nonparametric bootstrap (10{,}000 resamples) yields 95\% CI $[0.952, 0.995]$.

116	
117	\section{Router Training Details}
118	\label{app:router-details}
119	
120	We use Qwen3-1.7B for the router to avoid circular dependency with the Qwen2.5-7B data-generating model.
121	
122	Gradient accumulation is critical for the implicit router: with gradient accumulation of 1, the model fails to achieve meaningful routing discrimination (internal recall 26.7\%); with gradient accumulation of 8, the model becomes non-degenerate (internal recall above 50\% across all seeds). All main-text results use gradient accumulation 8.
123	
124	Cross-seed balanced accuracy is stable (57--59\%), but internal recall varies from 51.5\% to 81.8\%, reflecting the structurally thin learnable signal: when ${\sim}90\%$ of routing value is death avoidance, the non-death signal is inherently noisy. Deployed end-to-end, the trained router allocates 97.0\% of episodes to deterministic computation with 17.6\% death rate, and misrouting cost (death from internally-routed episodes) exceeds the benefit of correctly routing rare internal-preferred cases.

      "content": "\\paragraph{Cross-environment scope.}\nOur MATH and ALFWorld analyses (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}) provide cross-environment evidence but do not conclusively establish generalization. In MATH (no death penalty), the deterministic advantage vanishes with a directional reversal favoring internal reasoning ($+4.3$\\,pp, not statistically significant). In ALFWorld, the deterministic advantage stems from a capability gap rather than death avoidance. Environments with graded failure costs or intermediate penalty regimes remain unstudied.\n\n\\paragraph{Step-level mislabeling.} Episode-level labels may assign suboptimal routing decisions to individual steps; in particular, steps preceding a death event are labeled det-preferred even if the death was not foreseeable from those steps' observations. Training on pure-strategy trajectories but deploying on mixed policy also introduces a distribution shift \\citep{dagger-2011}.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope.}\nExtending the counterfactual methodology to environments with graded failure costs, embodied agents, or multi-turn dialogue systems would clarify how broadly death avoidance accounts for apparent routing signal. Step-level labeling with denser rewards and the logit--generation gap in the implicit router are natural next steps. We audit labeling quality in Appendix~\\ref{app:labeling}.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a publicly available text-based simulation environment, and does not involve human subjects or private data. 40	\end{tabular}
41	\caption{Episode-weighted mean scores on ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes). 95\% Bootstrap CIs. The oracle selects the higher-scoring strategy per episode; 96.3\% of its decisions match the always-deterministic strategy. $\dagger$\,4-seed mean.}
42	\label{tab:baselines}
43	\end{table}
44	
45	Table~\ref{tab:baselines} reports episode-weighted mean scores and death rates for four unconditional strategies. Always-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points, consistent with $\Delta_{\text{full}}$. The oracle, which selects the higher-scoring strategy per episode, improves only modestly to $-6.58$, with 96.3\% of its routing decisions matching always-deterministic. This \emph{oracle degeneracy} indicates that the optimal episode-level routing policy closely resembles unconditional deterministic computation---the routing signal is insufficient to improve upon simply always choosing the deterministic strategy. Random routing ($-22.38$) falls between the two pure strategies, with a death rate (27.8\%) proportional to its 50/50 allocation.
46	
47	Keyword-based routing fails structurally: match rates cluster at extremes (85.8\% or $<$5\%), precluding meaningful routing discrimination.
48	
49	\subsection{Router Training as Diagnostic}
50	\label{sec:router-diagnostic}
51	
52	A natural question is whether the episode-level counterfactual labels can train a step-level router. We investigate this as a diagnostic probe of label informativeness rather than a routing system proposal, testing two architectures.
53	
54	\paragraph{Classification head.} A linear head on LLM hidden representations, trained on episode-level labels, degenerates to always-deterministic (0\% internal recall) across all configurations (class weighting, focal loss, undersampling, temperature scaling).
55	
56	\paragraph{Implicit router.} We fine-tune Qwen3-1.7B to internalize routing decisions within its generation (Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}). Across four seeds, balanced accuracy is $58.1 \pm 0.8\%$---above chance but insufficient for practical routing, with seed-level variation (Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}) reflecting threshold sensitivity rather than genuine signal.
57	
58	\paragraph{End-to-end evaluation.} Deployed as an episode-level router (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}), the trained router scores $-10.70$ (95\% CI $[-16.33, -5.27]$), not statistically outperforming always-deterministic ($-9.47$, $[-14.71, -4.29]$; overlapping CIs). The episode-to-step granularity mismatch is the bottleneck: when DAF $\approx 90\%$, router headroom is limited to the ${\sim}10\%$ non-death signal.
59	
60	\paragraph{Data split.} Of 245 episodes, 95 non-tie labels are split 85/15 (81 train, 14 validation; 1734/306 steps). Baselines in Table~\ref{tab:baselines} report trajectory statistics; the router score ($-10.70$) comes from fresh environment interaction.
61	
62	\paragraph{Labeling quality.} We verify label quality in Appendix~\ref{app:labeling}.
63	
64	\subsection{Cross-Model Verification}
65	\label{sec:cross-model}
66	
67	To test whether the death avoidance pattern is model-specific, we run a mechanism verification pilot with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on two task types (\texttt{freeze} and \texttt{find-non-living-thing}, 5 episode pairs each, $N{=}10$). Llama-8B achieves lower absolute ScienceWorld scores than Qwen-2.5-7B, reflecting weaker task-level capability, but the death pattern direction is consistent across model families.
68	
69	On \texttt{freeze}, the internal strategy dies in 40\% of episodes versus 20\% for deterministic (Qwen: 55\%/25\%)---the same directional asymmetry. The single non-tie episode arises from death asymmetry: the internal strategy dies while deterministic survives, producing a det-preferred label consistent with the Qwen pattern. On \texttt{find-non-living-thing}, both strategies achieve 0\% death rate under Llama, matching Qwen exactly; the 3 det-preferred labels arise from non-death score differences.
70	
71	\begin{table}[t]
72	\centering
73	\small
74	\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccc@{}}
75	\toprule
76	Task & Model & D\%\textsubscript{int} & D\%\textsubscript{det} & $\Delta$D\% & D / I / N \\
77	\midrule
78	freeze & Qwen-7B & 55 & 25 & +30 & 6 / 0 / 14 \\
79	freeze & Llama-8B & 40 & 20 & +20 & 1 / 0 / 4 \\
80	\midrule
81	find-non-living & Qwen-7B & 0 & 0 & 0 & 16 / 4 / 20 \\
82	find-non-living & Llama-8B & 0 & 0 & 0 & 3 / 1 / 1 \\
83	\bottomrule
84	\end{tabular}
85	\caption{Cross-model death rate comparison. Llama pilot: $n{=}5$ episodes per task; Qwen: from Table~\ref{tab:per-task}. $\Delta$D\%: internal minus deterministic death rate. D/I/N: det-preferred / internal-preferred / no-preference labels.}
86	\label{tab:cross-model}
87	\end{table}
88	
89	Table~\ref{tab:cross-model} summarizes the comparison. This pilot ($N{=}10$ episode pairs) provides preliminary directional evidence only: the sample size precludes statistical claims. Nevertheless, observing the same death asymmetry direction in a different model family (Llama vs.\ Qwen) suggests that the pattern reflects a structural property of internal reasoning in interactive environments rather than a model-specific artifact.
```
